pressure on borrowing costs just as many companies
face refinancing needs. The impact on profitability
is material. We estimate that a 50bps increase in the
average interest rate on corporate debt would reduce
the net margin of non-financial corporations from 9.0%
to 8.6% in the US and from 6.6% to 6.2% in Europe,
ceteris paribus. With a 100bps increase, margins fall
further to 8.4% and 6.0%, respectively – a compression

of roughly 0.6pp or 60bps in both regions. The burden
is not evenly distributed. Exposure to higher rates is
concentrated in capital-intensive, real-asset sectors
with large debt stocks and persistent refinancing
requirements. Under a 100bps shock, the net margin of
US utilities falls from 19% to about 17%, while interest
coverage declines from 3.1x to 2.4x. For European
utilities, margins fall more sharply, from 10% to 7%, and
coverage drops from 2.0x to 1.5x. US telecoms also see

interest coverage fall substantially, from 4.5x to 2.8x,
compared with a decline from 2.0x to 1.7x in Europe
(see Figure 4). The implication is that the next phase
of pressure on corporate margins may come less from
the initial energy shock itself than from its second-
round effects on financing conditions. Companies that
entered this period with high capital intensity, limited
pricing power and large refinancing needs are therefore
increasingly exposed to a combination of weaker

operating leverage and higher debt-service costs.
The corporate response is increasingly one of
balance-sheet and industrial-footprint adjustment
rather than conventional cyclical hedging.  That is
consistent with companies treating both the higher-
rate environment and the emerging tariff architecture
as durable features of the operating landscape rather
than temporary shocks. Automakers are shifting the
focus of margin defense away from unit growth and

towards cost reductions, platform simplification and
lifecycle revenues. Chemicals groups are accelerating
capacity rationalization and redirecting investment
towards structurally advantaged geographies rather
than waiting for the cycle to turn. Metals producers,
meanwhile, are maintaining capital discipline even
as electrification-related demand increasingly strains
available supply. These strategies have a common
objective: preserving cash generation and balance-

sheet capacity through a period in which neither
demand nor financing conditions can be assumed to
normalize quickly. The emphasis is therefore moving
from maximizing volume growth to protecting returns
on capital, liquidity and strategic flexibility. This also
exposes a growing asymmetry in how markets are
pricing corporate risk. The upside associated with
the AI-linked cluster is being capitalized immediately
through stronger earnings expectations and investment

flows. By contrast, the downside from higher energy
costs, tariffs and interest rates is concentrated in the
other two clusters – and particularly in Europe, where
structural energy disadvantages, weaker pricing power
in parts of industry and greater sensitivity to external
trade shocks compound the effect. The result is not
simply a divergence between sectors, but a widening
gap between companies able to fund structural growth
and those forced to manage structural adjustment. That

distinction is likely to matter increasingly for margins,
investment and corporate balance sheets going
forward.

### Page 21

28 September 2026
Global business insolvencies are running +6% above
year-ago levels year-to-date, extending what is now
the sixteenth consecutive quarter of y/y increases
and leaving levels across most markets well above
pre-pandemic norms. Among the largest economies,
the US leads with a +19% surge, followed by Japan
(+7%), Germany (+7%) and France (+5%), while the UK
(-5%) and China (0%) are the notable exceptions. The
strain is especially visible at the top end of the corporate

spectrum: Insolvencies among firms with turnover
above EUR50mn hit 134 in Q2 2026, the third-highest
quarterly tally on record, bringing the count for the first
half of the year to 247 cases (+13% y/y), with Western
Europe accounting for nearly two-thirds of the total and
industrial sectors such as chemicals, automotive and
machinery equipment showing the sharpest deterioration.
Insolvencies are projected to rise by at least +4% globally
for the full year 2026 (the fifth consecutive year of

increases) before a tentative stabilization in 2027. North
America is set to be the largest regional contributor, with
US filings forecast at +7% but a candidate for upside
revision given strong first-half momentum; 27 countries
are already tracking above our full-year projections.
In Western Europe, the expected +2% increase in 2026
would be followed by a modest -3% decline in 2027, but
nine out of 17 markets are forecast within the -4%/+4%
range, pointing to quasi-stable insolvency levels that

remain historically elevated. Asia will see contrasting
trends, with most countries prolonging their rise, except
Australia, Singapore and Hong Kong. The full impact of
the Middle East shock has yet to feed through corporate
income statements: higher input and shipping costs,
weak(er) consumer sentiment, elevated selling-price
expectations and higher-for-longer interest rates all point
to persisting margin pressure in the coming quarters,
in particular for medium, small and very small firms.

Highly leveraged sectors are particularly vulnerable as
debt-servicing costs compound the profitability squeeze,
suggesting greater differentiation across countries and
industries depending on their exposure to war-related
spillovers and, conversely, their ability to benefit from
global tailwinds such as AI and defense spending.

Capital markets outlook:
Cruel (bond) summer coming to an end
Capital markets had a difficult summer as the energy
shock and renewed inflation concerns pushed
government bond yields sharply upwards to multi-year
highs. Equities proved more resilient: Strong earnings and
AI-related investment kept returns positive despite rising
rates, while credit spreads remained close to historical
tights, supported by solid corporate results. In currencies,

EURUSD stayed broadly range-bound, while emerging-
market assets benefited from improving fundamentals
but now offer less spread compensation. Private markets
are becoming more selective: Private equity remains
constrained by a record exit backlog, private debt
continues to deliver attractive income despite pockets of
stress, infrastructure is benefiting from the AI buildout and
real estate faces renewed refinancing pressure. Looking
ahead, we expect the summer repricing to prove orderly

rather than the start of a broader market break. As the
energy impulse fades in our baseline outlook, we see
scope for gradually declining long-term rates, while equity
returns should moderate as valuations normalize.
The summer selloff in long-term rates had three
synchronized drivers: the feedback loop between
energy prices and policy rate expectations, firmer
economic activity and a higher term premium. The
repricing of the rate path was the strongest driver. The

term premium was the swing factor capturing supply-
demand imbalances emerging over summer in a
government bonds market that is moved at the margin
by price-sensitive, leveraged investors that react to
tighter funding conditions. Firmer economic activity on
either side of the Atlantic acted as underlying upside
trend. But we think US late-cycle dynamics will resume
in 2027 as the energy impulse fades and growth returns
to potential. Markets will price rate cuts. Long-term

rates will anticipate lower growth, bringing 10y yields
to 4.5% in 2027 and 4.3% in 2028. The US term-premium
rebuild looks largely complete and is actively contained
by Treasury operations (buybacks and active Treasury
issuance). The hawkish Fed turn should itself re-anchor
the long end by restoring inflation-fighting credibility.

German 10y gov. Bond yield
The Eurozone sits earlier in the cycle. But its growth
impulse is moderate and there is no overshoot above
potential to justify holding policy above neutral for long.

We see markets pricing a reversal in mid-2027, easing
German 10y yields to 3.2% in 2027 and 3.0% in 2028. Both
curves bear-steepened through the summer. We now see
potential for further steepening, especially in the Eurozone
where the market-priced policy path is most excessively
tight. In both markets, the belly is the sweet spot on a six
to twelve month horizon. It captures some of the policy-
driven decline, stays supported by economic activity and

carries less term-premium risk than the long end. At current
levels, intermediate maturities show the best combination
of positive real returns and convexity.
Within the Eurozone the risk premium now sits in
France rather than Italy or Spain. The OAT-Bund spread
reached highest value since the Euro crisis (around
100bps) as markets began pricing a scenario in which the
new president (election in April/May 2027) will struggle
to gain a majority in the parliament, creating further

fiscal uncertainty. The near-term risk stays skewed wider,
and 130bps is reachable if political fragility deepens to
levels not yet seen. BTP spreads stay contained, but the
tightening impulse is missing as NGEU support fades, and
upcoming elections are an upside risk we would treat as
temporary. There is no systemic fragmentation risk within
EGBs as yield movement remain synchronized. Political
fragility, not market fragmentation, will be the theme of

the coming months.
EURUSD traded within a relatively narrow range of
1.14 to 1.18 during the last quarter, largely continuing
the pattern observed in previous quarters. Traditional
drivers such as the expected interest rate differential
and valuation metrics suggest that this range-bound
behavior is likely to persist. Within this range, short-
term exchange rate movements were primarily driven
by developments in the US, whereas events such as the

recent German elections had only a limited impact on
the currency pair. We expect this pattern to continue.
With the US midterm elections likely to increase political
uncertainty, we believe EURUSD could move closer to the
upper end of its recent trading range by year-end.
Equity markets: the 2026 earnings boost gives way
to normalization. Equities have held up through the
summer oil shock, leaving year-to-date total returns at
+13% in the US and +10% for Europe. Earnings did the

work: 12-month forward EPS growth runs near +20%
in the US, +16% in the EMU and above +30% in EMs.
But valuation dispersion is the widest in a decade: The
US trades at 19.4x forward earnings against a 20-year
average of 16.6x; the Eurozone at 16.4x and EM at 9.9x.
Valuations argue for selectivity rather than retreat:
Earnings should keep delivering the equity risk premium,

but with PEs above average in the US and Japan, we
expect the valuation premium to deflate there as growth
slows and macro risks rise, while Europe and EMs –

where expectations are lowest and the downside most
discounted – offer similar earnings sensitivity at a lower
price. We stay constructive on equities and expect Q4
to consolidate rather than extend the rally, with returns
converging towards the long-term equity risk premium
as multiples normalize. At year end, full-year 2026 total
returns should reach +14% for the US and +15% for the
Europe, before a more normal year in 2027 with +8% and
+9%, respectively. Double-digit EPS growth carries the

forecast (US: +32%/+16%/+16% over FY1-FY3; Eurozone:
+22%/+12%/+12%). US earnings revisions (+7.1% since
July) outrun nominal GDP only thanks to technology,
~30% of the index; ex-tech, the S&P 500 tracks the
economy, so the market carries the downside if AI capex
or monetization disappoints – growth, not inflation, is
the risk. Eurozone consensus prices +14.7% EPS growth
off +4.6% nominal GDP , but macro upgrades, lower
valuations and already negative sentiment support it,

while Japan looks hyped after a +20%. With positioning
crowded and implied volatility below average,
convertibles and hedges are compelling to retain equity
and earnings upside while tailoring the downside to
tolerance.
Credit markets have remained broadly resilient
through 2026, with spreads holding near the tight
end of their long-term range. In investment grade (IG),
upgrades continue to outnumber downgrades, though
the net upgrade trend has slowed from its 2025 peak.

Solid growth is offsetting the higher interest burden,
holding credit metrics: Interest coverage went below
its long-term median but stabilized above 6x in the
US and 8x in Europe, while net leverage remains near
cycle highs. In high yield (HY), global default rates have
stabilized, albeit at a level still above their long-run
average. The market has differentiated, reflecting tight
spreads yet above-average default rates: BB and B
credits have outperformed, supported by high carry and

fading recession risk, while CCC spreads remain elevated
and are likely to stay so as the weakest issuers face the
highest refinancing costs in a higher-for-longer rate
environment.

EUR Investment Grade spread
EUR High Yield spread (rhs)
In the US, earnings growth and supportive technicals
have underpinned the market, though heavy technology
issuance is drawing investor attention.  IG gross supply

reached nearly USD1.5trn year-to-date, up 30% on
2025, while net issuance almost doubled. Demand has
moderated from the high of the start of the year, but new
issuance book coverage above 3x suggests the market
can still absorb a sizeable calendar. With technology
set to account for around a quarter of new issuance
through 2026, its share of the IG index could rise towards
15% in 2030. Heavier supply against adequate demand
argues for drift rather than dislocation: We expect US IG

at 85bps by end-2026 and 90bps in 2027–28, with HY at
290bps, 315bps and 310bps. Euro credit is comparatively
insulated from this technology concentration and offers
a diversification option. Technology represents only 3% of
the Euro IG index, which should limit spillovers from tech-
specific volatility. Current tight spreads are driven by ‘yield’
demand, and are likely to continue in a higher-for-longer
rates environment. However, increasing rate volatility

started to weigh on spreads in the near term. We see EUR
IG at around 85bps in 2027–28 and HY at approximately
300bps and 295bps.
Emerging markets remain resilient to global shocks.
However, the path for EM sovereign spreads is
asymmetrically skewed towards widening, while energy
volatility and Fed hiking remain the drivers for EMs
locally. EM fundamentals continue to strengthen, with
sovereign rating trends clearly shifting toward upgrades,
supported by successful debt restructurings, IMF-backed

policy discipline and improving monetary policy credibility
across the asset class. The structural growth premium that
EMs hold over developed markets remains intact and is
expected to widen further, supporting debt sustainability
and increasing the resilience to higher energy prices and
US rates volatility. Robust fundamentals, combined with
sustained investor inflows, have pushed EM sovereign
spreads to multi-year tight levels, compressing the rating-

adjusted spread premium versus US corporates close to
negative territory. From current multi-year tight levels,
spreads are expected to widen modestly by year-end.
With spreads offering limited additional pick-up, EM
local-currency assets appear relatively more attractive
at current yield levels. Given their correlation with
US Treasury yields and the ongoing EM central bank
cycle, yields are expected to move broadly sideways,
reaching 630bps by year-end. While valuations look

more compelling in local currency markets, the recent
environment is less favorable as Fed policy expectations
and oil-price volatility remain the key drivers of local
currency performance.

### Page 27

28 September 2026
27
EM equities are the cheapest way into the AI value
chain. Consensus EPS growth of +72%, +23% and +10%
over FY1-FY3 is driven by semiconductors, and AI supports
around a quarter of Asian growth. Yet the market still
prices EM semis at a discount to US peers: Their combined
USD4trn market capitalization is below Nvidia alone,
and technology weighs 21% in MSCI ACWI, of which only
3.5% is for EM semis – despite their crucial role and early

monetization. SK Hynix and Samsung trade near 4x PE,
with new long-term contracts extending the outlook
and securing payback, whereas US semis command
three times the multiple for twice the growth. The cycle
has limits: We estimate AI capex to be roughly halfway
through and expect it to peak above USD1trn in 2028,
when normalizing returns on equity would imply a 20-
45% PE discount that our target already anticipates. We
forecast MSCI EM total returns of +31% in 2026, +12% in

2027 and +7% in 2028 – ahead of developed markets next
year, fading only as the semiconductor cycle matures.
Across the USD16.7trn private capital industry, two
forces are doing most of the sorting. Rates, having risen
sharply again on both sides of the Atlantic rather than
easing as underwritten, are pushing financing costs back
toward levels many portfolios were not built for, and AI is
rewriting which businesses deserve a premium and which
do not. Each corner of private capital wears this pressure

differently. Private equity is stuck at the exit, sitting on
a record backlog of unsold companies as chastened
investors keep fundraising in a multi-year drought. Private
debt still pays its keep, with direct lending returning
almost 11% last year, but cracks are showing among
smaller funds and the most AI-exposed borrowers, where
write-downs are creeping higher. Infrastructure is the
outlier success story, its 12-13% annual return leaning on
the USD5-7trn the world is expected to spend this decade

wiring up data centers for the AI boom. Real estate‘s
fragile recovery is now at risk of stalling as financing costs
move higher again instead of easing, and AI‘s dampening
effect on office demand leaves the sector doubly exposed.
The thread running through all four is dispersion. Capital
is flowing to contracted, well-covered, AI-aligned assets
and fleeing everything else, widening the gap between
winners and laggards to levels rarely seen.
Private equity‘s story in 2026 is increasingly one of

exits, or the lack of them. US buyout funds are still sitting
on some 13,300 unsold companies, most acquired in the
frothy days of 2020-21 at prices that assumed multiples
would keep rising. Instead, across US buyout deals,
multiple expansion contributed 37.3% of value creation in
the 2011-13 vintages but just 0.5% in 2020-22, with EBITDA
margin expansion and revenue growth now doing almost
all the work (Figure 9). That is a much harder way to earn

a return, and it shows up directly in the exit numbers. US
exits fell -46.3% in the second quarter, kept alive mainly
by a handful of large sales rather than a broad clearing
of the backlog, while dealmaking itself dropped -37.5%
as the Fed reopened the door to rate hikes and software,
previously the industry‘s favored sector, fell -65.7% on
AI-driven concerns about its growth outlook. With so
little cash flowing back to investors, distributions remain

thin and the incentive to hold rather than sell persists.
Europe tells a related story, deal value rose +6.9% in
the second quarter, but its own deals-to-exit ratio has
climbed from 2.4x to 2.9x, an early sign that the same
operational reckoning may be coming. The lesson on
both sides of the Atlantic is the same: Returns now have
to be built company by company rather than bought
through re-rating, and only sponsors who can genuinely
grow revenue and margins will convert their backlog into

distributions. We expect private equity to return 7-9% this
year, rising to 11-12% in 2027 and 2028 as the exit market
gradually reopens.

Private debt has spent 2026 absorbing shocks it was
largely built to withstand. Oil spikes and rate scares are

not new for this asset class. The summer‘s Hormuz-driven
energy shock pushed spreads a little wider, but the move
has been modest. Spreads remain close to historic lows,
and leveraged loan defaults, while ticking up, are still only
a small drag on returns. In this environment we expect
defaults to normalize gradually as energy prices and
rate expectations settle back down. Some of the recent
concern bundles together several separate issues – retail-

vehicle redemptions, loan quality, software exposure and
fund-wrapper design – and treating them as one story
overstates the risk. The more interesting divergence is in
valuations rather than credit quality. Private NAVs have
held steady, while listed vehicles trade near 17% discounts,
and payment-in-kind income, now around 8% of BDC
investment income, is edging up among the weakest
borrowers, whose interest coverage is squeezed by still-
high floating coupons. The sensible approach, as in most

elsewhere in the portfolio. We expect spreads to hold
around 550bps through year-end, cruising at similar levels
through 2027 and 2028 as the asset class settles into a
steadier, income-led phase.
Infrastructure is expected to sustain strong
performance, underpinned by AI buildout as a growth
lever. Data centers continue to dominate the deal market
this year. Despite strong new-build across major regions,
demand keeps outstripping supply (Figure 14), holding

vacancy at record lows while rents keep growing strongly.
With power, permitting, and land constraints sustaining
the scarcity, the economics stay attractive for ready-to-use
data centers contracted long-term to investment-grade
counterparties. Two tailwinds are strengthening the case.
First, the token economics are getting more compelling:
Usage keeps surging despite significant efficiency gains
as workloads shift from chatbots to agentic, reasoning-

heavy models and the paradigm grows more compute-
intensive. Token prices are stabilizing at the frontier
even as serving costs keep falling, supporting provider
margins. Hyperscaler AI revenue is now beginning to cover
the capex depreciation, pointing to a more sustainable
cycle. Second, rising data sovereignty demands across
regions add a further durable tailwind. We continue to
see infrastructure as a strong play in the AI supercycle –
the foundation on which the whole AI stack is built – with

tangible, contracted earnings rather than speculative
upside.

### Page 29

28 September 2026
29
The income-led recovery in real estate continues,
but higher long-end yields weigh on pricing and
refinancing. Liquidity is returning ahead of pricing:
Investment volumes are up 21% y/y in the US and 10%
in Europe, drifting back to pre-Covid norms, while prices
are stabilizing and cap rates are broadly flat. Values
remain 14-16% below the 2022 peak in aggregate on
both sides of the Atlantic, with US offices down more
than 40% and Europe offices down over 20%, against

high-single-digit drawdowns in US retail and industrial
and European residential. Fundamentals are supportive:
shrinking new supply and resilient occupier demand
should underpin a progressive, income-led recovery, but
the path is gradual, with no sector expected to regain its
peak before 2028 and offices to barely move (Figure 15).
Rates are the constraint. With 10y yields at multi-decade
highs on renewed inflation pressure and fiscal concerns,
private appraisal cap rates still sit near 2021 levels.

Marks have yet to absorb the move, so further outward
yield shift and headwinds on refinancing and liquidity lie
ahead.

## Document 193: August CPI – Came in on our expectations (2026-10-04)

Source PDF: all report pdf/August CPI – Came in on our expectations (2026-10-04).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 16
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#inflation #australia #rba #economicbulletinsaustralia #inflation
August CPI – Came in on our expectations
By Justin Smirk & Neha Sharma
15:27 September 30 2026 Share
August Monthly CPI: Headline 0.4%mth/4.0%yr, Trimmed Mean 0.2%mth/3.6%yr. Market
services ex volatile –0.4%mth/3.3%yr.

The CPI rose 0.4% in August, spot on Westpac’s expectation but a bit softer than market
expectations of 0.5%. Due to base effects the annual pace picked up to 4.0%yr from 3.5%yr.

### Page 2

Key factors driving the headline result were auto fuels (14.8%); games, toys & hobbies (10.2%) with
rents providing a solid base (0.3%) while some offset was provided by both domestic (–4.9%) and
international holiday travel & accommodation (–1.5%).
Trimmed mean (TM) inflation printed 0.2% again bang on Westpac’s expectation while the market
was slightly higher at 0.3%. The annual pace was flat at 3.6%yr, the third month in a row at this pace,
while the six-month annualise pace was steady at 3.9%yr.

Key factors driving the TM print were: rents (0.3%) and new dwelling purchases (0.2%). The TM
distribution ranged from –0.38% to 0.82%.
Today’s inflation update revealed price discounting for some durable goods as retail demand softens.
We would expect to see a bounce from these categories in the following month. There was also
offsetting strength elsewhere in durable good so at least for now, the momentum continues to
suggest some near term upside risk to our current inflation estimates.

Nevertheless, as the TM came in on expectations and based on a first quick pass at updating the
quarterly profile, we see little pressure to revise our current Q3 estimates of 1.3%qtr for the CPI and
0.9%qtr for the TM.
The RBA remains alert to near term inflationary risks. We are expecting the momentum in TM
inflation to remain well above the top of the target band through to year-end underpinning our
expectations for the RBA to raise rates again in November.

### Page 3

August CPI in more detail
Following the bumper read in July, the monthly CPI moderated in August rising 0.4%mth. This was in
line with our expectations and slightly softer than the market consensus. This took the year-ended pace
to 4.0%yr. On a seasonally adjusted basis the CPI rose 0.7%mth highlighting the impact of seasonal
sales in August.

They key drivers behind the monthly lift were automotive fuel, up 14.8%mth, contributing 0.5ppts to the
headline figure. The surge resulted from the ending of the fuel excise discount on August 3 and rising
global energy prices. With crude oil prices still elevated due to the conflict in the middle east, and
growing shortages of refined products due to both the middle east conflict and Ukrainian assaults on
Russian refining capacity, auto fuel is expected to remain an inflationary impulse at least until the end
of 2026.

The monthly TM also softened, rising 0.2%mth compared to the 0.5%mth read from July. The result
was again in line with our expectations, and very close at two decimal places (0.24%mth vs
0.23%mth), but softer than the market's view. The year-ended pace held at 3.6%yr for the third
consecutive month, although it continued to ease marginally at the second decimal place. In contrast,
the six-month annualised pace has continued to trend higher since April and is currently running at
3.9%yr.

### Page 4

There is a possibility that as the ABS continues to grapple with seasonal adjustment issues we will see
revisions to the TM estimates. Last July also saw a strong 0.48%mth read which eased to 0.29%mth in
August. This is not too dissimilar from the 0.48%mth and 0.24%mth reads for 2026.

We are expecting core inflation to start to moderate as we get to year end but our current estimate for
the month of December still has the TM running at a 3.3% in the year while the six-month annualised
pace is still a chunky 3.1%. Applying a trim on the seasonally adjusted 3m/3m expenditure classes
yields a TM of 0.9%qtr currently. This is in line with our current Q3 TM estimate.

Other measures of underlying inflation told a similar story, easing on a monthly basis but holding steady
on a year-ended basis. Market services ex volatiles fell –0.4%mth to be 3.3%yr higher. Excluding both
volatiles & holidays saw a 0.2%mth/4.1%yr lift.

Most market services components eased in the month (including on a seasonally adjusted basis) but
annual growth across this group as a whole remained above the RBA's 2-3% target band. The near-
term momentum (three-month annualised) is also quite strong across several components of services.
Of note include deposit and loan facilities (11.2%mth), sports participation (10.9%), clothing repair
(7.0%), restaurant meals (6.8%) and other household services (5.2%). We are closely watching market
services as this is where inflationary risks, outside of energy, appear to be most persistent.

Due to its sizable weight in the CPI, and the fact price changes here tend to be less volatile than many
other categories, so they almost always appear in the TM estimates, housing prices remain a key
focus. Rents rose 0.3%mth in August, in line with our expectations, to be up 3.6%yr. Since April, the
seasonally adjusted rents series has been running lower than the non-seasonally adjusted series. We
are expecting rents to hold around this pace till year end before moderating modestly as we move
through 2027.

New dwellings inflation was 0.2%mth in August (softer than our expectation for a 0.4%mth rise). Since
peaking in May, monthly price growth has slowed in each successive month, potentially reflecting
increasing difficulty for developers in passing through higher costs amid softer conditions in established
housing markets and weaker demand in the face of higher interest rates. The annual pace looks to
have topped out. It was at 5.8%yr in June easing to 5.4%yr currently with the three-month annualised
pace at 3.7%. We expect dwelling price inflation to continue easing through to the end of 2027 as
falling established house prices limit the ability of builders to pass on rising construction costs. There is
a medium-term risk that as conditions improve builders may raise prices more aggressively to claw
back margins.

### Page 5

Overall, the breadth of inflation moderated in August. The share of the basket running above 3%yr
eased from 45% to 42%, with the share below 2%yr increasing from 38% to 41%.

### Page 6

Key surprises in the month:
Upside surprises

Downside surprises
Insurance surprised to the upside (0.7%mth vs 0.3%mth) after a run of weaker-than-usual reads over
the past two months.
Motor vehicle repair costs rose a solid 0.8% against our 0.3%mth expectations, with spare parts and
accessories also up 0.3%mth vs –0.4%mth.
Footwear rose 1.7%mth against our expectations for a -2.9%mth decline, the gains were broad-
based across women, men & children.
Household goods surprised to the upside. Household appliances, utensils and tools rose 1.9%mth
against our flat expectations, meanwhile household textiles fell –1.0%mth against our expectations
for a –5.0%mth fall. Furniture and furnishings also fell by less than expected –0.6%mth vs –1.3%mth.
Recreation and culture surprised to the upside (–1.0%mth vs –1.5%mth), owing to a 1.4%mth lift in
other recreation, sport & culture against our expectations for a flat read on the back of an outsized
gain in games, toys & hobbies (10.2%mth vs 2.5%mth). Holiday travel was marginally less weak than
expected (–3.3% vs –3.5%mth).

### Page 7

Food was the key downside miss falling –0.1%mth against our expectations for a 0.2%mth lift. Meat
& seafood (–0.9%mth vs 0.5%mth) and fruit & vegetables (–1.2%mth vs 0.1%mth) were the key
areas of weakness.
Audio, visual and computing equipment and services came in weaker-than-expected (0.7%mth vs
1.2%mth), owing to a softer 0.6%mth lift in computing services (3.0%mth expected). This was partly
balanced by a stronger than expected lift in computing equipment (0.9%mth vs –0.5%mth). The latter

is where we are most likely to see the impact of the AI boom in the CPI, due to computer chip
inflation, other than construction cost inflation for new dwellings.
Motor vehicles came in lower at –0.4%mth vs –0.1%mth which could be the start of price pressures
associated with the rapid expansion of the share of Chinese cars in the Australian market.
Automotive fuel was also slightly softer at 14.8%mth vs 16.7%mth.
Garments and accessories were both softer. Garments fell –1.9%mth against our expectations for a

0.2%mth rise, meanwhile accessories came in at 0.1%mth vs 0.5%mth expected.

### Page 8

What does this mean for the RBA?
Yesterday, post the RBA rate hike and monetary policy decision statement, Westpac changed its view
from a November follow up rate hike being a risk to being the base case. The post-meeting statement
was notable for its emphasis on the Middle East conflict, noting that “global energy prices are now
much higher than had been assumed in the August forecasts”. In addition, RBA’s liaison suggests firms
are passing this on or planning to do so. The pass-through was characterised as partial, and additional

to domestically driven inflation arising from capacity pressures. We read this as implying that the RBA
is concerned about upside risks to underlying inflation in Q4 of this year, as well as Q3.

### Page 9

Related articles
Our current forecast of 0.9%qtr for the September quarter TM stands post today’s release and
represents a step up from 0.8% in the June quarter holding the annual pace at 3.6%yr. Our current
forecast for the Q4 TM is 0.7%qtr/3.3%yr but given the move in energy prices, and anecdotes of firms
now passing on those rising costs, we too see upside risks to this estimate.

As such, we hold to the view that the RBA will follow up with another rate hike in November. For more
information on the RBA decision please see Luci Ellis’s note “RBA hikes in September, November
follow-up now expected".

### Page 2

forecasts are based are reasonable, the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially

2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The Cotality home value index, covering all of Australia,
fell 1.1% in September with downward revisions to the
previous two months again shaving a further 0.4% off prices.
The correction has now seen prices decline 5.2% from their

March peak. Annual growth is flat.
The price correction remains broad, Cotality reporting 97% of
capital city suburbs experienced falls over the three months
to September, Darwin the only city to record gains. Sydney
and Melbourne continue to see more pronounced declines,
albeit moderating a touch, particularly in Melbourne.
Against this, declines in Brisbane, Adelaide and Perth have
accelerated over the last few months. Across segments, unit
prices and lower price tiers continue to track slightly milder

falls than houses and top tiers.
Seasonal variations accentuated price weakness through winter
but are now neutral. The peak-to-trough price decline nationally
is slightly milder at 4.6% in seasonally adjusted terms. Unless
specified, figures in titles, tables and the commentary below are
in non-seasonally adjusted terms, consistent with the ‘headline’
figures reported by Cotality. However, figures shown in charts
are in seasonally adjusted terms.

Preliminary estimates of turnover show rolling 3mth volumes
down 3.9%qtr in September and 17.4%yr with previous
estimates revised 1-2ppts lower. The monthly pattern
suggests small gains in July and August have given way to
another sizeable fall in September.
Looking at the price performance across different jurisdictions:
• Sydney prices dropped 1.4% fall in September, monthly
declines averaging 1.6% over the previous four months,
tracking a 17% annualised pace of decline since May. All

segments and tiers recorded falls in Q3 but with top and
middle tier houses notably weaker.
• Melbourne prices fell 0.7%mth, a slower pace than other
capitals. This likely reflects the weaker starting point coming
into the correction, prices already back to the levels seen in
mid-2021. As elsewhere, 'top tier' houses prices are leading
the decline. 'Low tier' unit prices mostly held their ground in
Q3, prices in this sub-segment already plumbing 10yr lows.

• Brisbane was the weakest capital city price-wise, recording
a 1.5% drop in September with previous monthly declines
also revised down heavily (August from 1% to 1.6% and
July from 1.2% to 1.7%). The combined effect has taken
the rolling 3mth change to –4.7%qtr from –2.7%qtr last
month and –0.6%qtr in July. At 5.9%yr, annual growth is
still positive but losing altitude quickly, having run at 18%yr
as recently as May. Sellers have been slow to respond to

the sudden stop with total listings up over 50% vs a year

• Adelaide dwelling prices fell 1.3%mth, the August decline

milder declines in NSW (–0.7%) and Victoria (0.3%). Annual
prices growth is in the 3.4-6.3%yr range.

Our recently released Housing Pulse report (here) provides
a deeper diver into the situation in Australia's housing
markets. While the absence of selling pressure, still tight
supply-demand balance and sentiment mix suggests the
correction was still relatively 'orderly' through the September
quarter, the latest monthly data shows momentum remains
unambiguously negative.
That said, there are clearly more headwinds to come. The
RBA latest interest rate rise and what now looks to be a

more likely prospect of a follow-up increase at its meeting in
early November will impact in coming months. Next week's
consumer sentiment update will show how the change, and
the latest round of weak updates, is shaping housing-related
sentiment. Either way, momentum looks set to remain weak
into year-end and, barring a significantly improved inflation
and interest rate outlook, early 2027.

## Document 236: ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD_ October 5-9

Source PDF: all report pdf/ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD_ October 5-9.pdf
Report source: Substack Reports
Date: 2026-10-04
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD: October 5-9
Date:    13 hours ago
ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD: October 5-9

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD:
October 5-9
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 4 Oct 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Monica Sauro / Unsplash

### Page 3

Most of last week's economic indicator reports were strong: Initial jobless
claims fell to near a 57-year low, ADP private payrolls rose 90,000 in
September, and the ISM M-PMI was in expansion for a ninth straight
month. Friday's employment report was the exception. Nonfarm payrolls
rose just 29,000 in September, well below expectations, while July and
August were revised down by a combined 60,000. The unemployment
rate edged up to 4.2%.
Odds of a 25bps hike in the federal funds rate (FFR) at the October 27-28

FOMC meeting fell to 22%, with odds of one at the December meeting at
67%. Odds of two hikes by December is at just 3%. In other words, the
market still expects a rate hike this year, though it has all but ruled out
two. However, we still think the Fed ought to hike at least enough to
reverse last year's three 25bps "insurance" cuts. September's hike
reversed one, which leaves two more to go.
This week's data calendar is light, with Wednesday's FOMC minutes the

main event. Here's more:
(1) FOMC minutes. September's FOMC meeting minutes (Wed) should
reveal how much support the Committee has for tightening beyond what
the dot plot in the September meeting’s Summary of Economic
Projections implies.
The median projection of meeting participants showed the FFR at 4.1% at
the end of this year and next, i.e., one more 25bps hike in 2026 followed
by a hold lasting throughout 2027 (chart). Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said the

September hike "removed a dose of accommodation," implying that the
FFR remains below the neutral rate. The median longer-run neutral rate
estimate in September’s dot plot rose to 3.25%, a post-pandemic high.

### Page 4

The FFR futures market’s prediction is more hawkish than the Fed
officials’ median. FFR futures imply 3.3 rate hikes over the next 12 months
and 1.7 over the next six months (chart).
The 2-year Treasury yield is at 4.78%, well above the 4.00% top of the
FFR target range, with 12-month futures at 4.70% (chart).

### Page 5

(2) PMIs. S&P Global's flash NM-PMI jumped to 58.7 in September from
56.5 in August, suggesting that ISM's NM-PMI (Mon), which was 55.4 in
August, remained strong last month (chart). Services prices-paid
components deserve close attention, as the supercore PCED inflation rate
has been rising since October 2025.
(3) ADP employment & jobless claims. ADP's weekly employment
reading (Tue), covering the weeks ended September 12 and 19, follows a
rise in the four-week average to 20,000 for the week ended September 5,

up from a late-July bottom of 8,250 (chart). That pace closely matched
September's ADP private payrolls gain of 90,000.

### Page 6

Initial jobless claims (Thu) follow last week's 197,000, the lowest since
July 18. The four-week average fell to 200,000. Continuing claims
dropped to 1,701,000 for the week ended September 18, the lowest since
March 2023, with the four-week average at 1,739,000. The low level of
initial claims confirmed September's low unemployment rate of 4.2%
(chart).
(4) Global. Eurozone retail sales ex autos fell 0.6% m/m in July, following
June's record high (chart). August's data (Tue) should show whether that

was just a blip.

### Page 7

Bank of England Governor Andrew Bailey may signal whether rising
energy prices warrant a hike when he speaks at the Central Bank of
Turkey's conference (Thu), after three Monetary Policy Committee
members voted for one in September. The markets put the odds of a
November hike at 88%, with roughly 66bps of additional tightening priced
in for 2027.
Canada's employment report for September (Fri) follows August's 41,700
job loss, with the unemployment rate holding at 6.4% (chart).

## Document 316: RBAは３会合ぶりの利上げ再開も、先行きは慎重姿勢 ～インフレ高止まりを警戒して追加利上げに含みもペース鈍化か､豪ドル相場はどうなる？～

Source PDF: all report pdf/RBAは３会合ぶりの利上げ再開も、先行きは慎重姿勢 ～インフレ高止まりを警戒して追加利上げに含みもペース鈍化か､豪ドル相場はどうなる？～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-09-29
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Asia Trends

2026 年 9 月 29 日

1/5 
RBA は３会合ぶりの利上げ再開も、先行きは慎重姿勢 
～インフレ高止まりを警戒して追加利上げに含みもペース鈍化か､豪ドル相場はどうなる？～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ オーストラリア準備銀行（ＲＢＡ）は９月 28～29 日の金融政策委員会で、政策金利を 25bp
引き上げて4.60％とすることを全会一致で決定した。 利上げは３会合ぶりで、 年初以降では
４回目となる。 背景には、 ７月のインフレ率が前年比＋3.5％と 13 ヵ月連続で目標レンジ （２
～３％） を上回り、 コアインフレ率も高止まりするなど、 物価上昇圧力が根強いことがある。
一方、失業率は 4.6％まで上昇したものの、雇用者数や労働参加率は高水準で、労働需給に
はなおひっ迫感が残る。 
○ ＲＢＡは声明で、イラン情勢の長期化によるエネルギー価格上昇、ＡＩ関連需要を背景とし
たテクノロジー関連財の値上がり、国内の供給制約などを理由に「インフレ率は依然として
高過ぎる」と強調した。景気や住宅市場には減速の兆しがみられるものの、インフレを適切
な期間内に目標へ戻すためには一段の金融引き締めが妥当と判断し、必要に応じて追加利上
げを行う可能性に言及した。 
○ ブロック総裁も、インフレ圧力が想定以上に長期化する可能性を指摘する一方、今後は「慎
重な引き締め」を進める姿勢を示し、インフレが落ち着けば追加利上げは不要になるとの考
えも示した。今回の利上げは市場予想通りで、声明はタカ派的だった一方、総裁会見には慎
重さもみられた。このため、米国の追加利上げ観測による米ドル高も重なり、当面の豪ドル
相場は対米ドル、対円ともに上値の重い展開が続く可能性がある。

【ＲＢＡは３会合ぶりの利上げ決定､これにより年明け以降の利上げは４回目に】 
オーストラリア準備銀行（ＲＢＡ）は、９月 28～29 日の日程で開催した定例の金融政策委員会
において、政策金利であるキャッシュレートを25bp 引き上げて4.60％とすることを決定した。Ｒ
ＢＡは、 ２月の定例会合において２年３ヶ月ぶりの利上げに舵を切り、 ５月の定例会合まで３会合
連続の利上げを実施するなど、引き締め姿勢を強めてきた。その後、６月会合、８月会合と金利を
据え置く様子見姿勢に転じたものの、今回は３会合ぶり、４回目の利上げとなる。

### Page 2

2/5 
事前の市場予想では、ＲＢＡが利上げに動くとの見方が大宗を占めた。背景には、インフレ圧力
の根強さがある。 直近７月のインフレ率
は前年比＋3.5％と前月 （同＋3.8％） か
ら鈍化したものの、13 ヵ月連続で目標
レンジ （２～３％） を上回る伸びとなっ
た（図１）。 前月比も＋1.02％と前月 （同
▲0.06％） から３ヵ月ぶりの上昇に転じ
ており、 原油高の再燃によるエネルギー
価格の上昇に加え、 生鮮品をはじめとす
る食料品価格も上昇するなど、 生活必需
品を中心にインフレ圧力が強まったこ
とが影響した。 一方で、 コアインフレ率
（トリム平均ベース） は前年比＋3.6％と前月 （同＋3.6％）から横ばいで推移したが、 こちらも10
ヵ月連続で目標レンジを上回る伸びで推移した。前月比も＋0.49％と前月（同＋0.28％）から上昇
ペースが加速しており、財・サービスともに上昇基調を強めている。月次の物価統計のなかでＲＢ
Ａが比較的注視していた物価変動の大きい財と観光を除いたベースは前年比＋3.8％と前月（同＋
4.2％） から鈍化して７ヵ月ぶりの低い伸びとなった。 しかし、 前月比は＋0.79％と前月 （同＋0.02％）
から上昇ペースが加速しており、インフレ圧力の根強さを示唆する動きもみられた。 
一方で、ＲＢＡは、物価安定と完全雇用の維持という「デュアルマンデート」に基づいた金融政
策を運営している。こうしたなか、８月の失業率（季調済）は4.6％と前月（4.5％）から0.1pt 悪
化して 2021 年 11 月以来の高水準となった（図２）。失業者数が前月比＋2.8 万人と前月（同＋0.5
万人）から３ヵ月連続で増加し、パート
タイム、フルタイム問わず求職者数が増
加したことが影響した。一方で、就業者
数は前月比＋3.9 万人と前月 （同▲1.6 万
人）から増加に転じており、フルタイム
就業者数（同▲0.6 万人）は４ヵ月ぶり
の減少に転じたものの、 パートタイム就
業者数（同＋4.6 万人）の増加が全体を
押し上げた。地域別でも、大都市部のみ
ならず、資源関連産業が集積する西オー
ストラリア州でも増加し、商品市況の上
昇が雇用環境を下支えしている。 さらに、 雇用環境の底堅さを反映して、 労働力人口は前月比＋6.8
万人、労働参加率も 67.1％と 16 ヵ月ぶりの高水準となるなど、労働市場への参入意欲の高さがう
かがえる。総労働時間は増加する一方で不完全雇用率は低下しており、 労働需給にひっ迫感が残る。
このため、金融市場ではＲＢＡによる利上げ観測が高まった。

3/5

【｢インフレ率が高過ぎる｣との認識をあらためて強調し､追加利上げの可能性に含み】 
会合後に公表された声明文では、今回の決定は「全会一致であった」として、すべての政策委員
が利上げを志向したことを明らかにした。そのうえで、物価動向について「 依然高水準にある」、
「８月会合時点で指摘した上振れリスクの一部が顕在化している」 との認識を示した。 具体的には
「イラン情勢が長期化し、世界のエネルギー価格は８月会合時点の想定を大幅に上回っている 」、
「ＡＩ（人工知能）関連需要を理由にテクノロジー関連財の価格も急上昇している」 、 「国内の供給
能力もひっ迫している」 ことを挙げた。 そして、 「企業部門はコスト上昇圧力に直面しており、 すで
に財・サービス価格を引き上げている、ないし引き上げを検討している」 、 「短期的なインフレ期待
も高止まりしている」として、 「足元のインフレ率は想定を上回った」とした。 
実体経済について 「生産の伸びは鈍化
しているが、４-６月はわずかに想定を
上回った」、「個人消費は想定通り緩やか
に鈍化している」 、 「大半の州都で住宅価
格は下落し（図３）、 新規住宅ローンも減
少している」とした。さらに、労働市場
について 「ここ数ヵ月はおおむね想定通
り緩和しており、 先行指標も安定してい
る」 としつつ、 「企業の設備投資と債務は
増加している」とした。先行きについて
は「不確実性が引き続き高い」として、
その理由に「イラン情勢は収束せず、インフレ率が想定を上回る一方、景気動向が想定を下回る可
能性もある」 、 「世界的な原油供給の混乱によりエネルギー価格とインフレ率に引き続き上昇圧力が
かかっている」ことを挙げた。 そして、 「不確実性が長期化すれば、 国内外の景気が一段と弱まる可
能性もある」との認識を示した。 
足元の世界経済について 「世界的なＡＩ関連投資の押し上げ効果がイラン情勢の悪影響を上回っ
ている」 とする一方、豪州経済については 「生産性の伸びの弱さが潜在成長率の重しとなっており、
住宅市場の低迷が経済に及ぼす影響にも不確実性が残る」 との見方を示した。政策運営については
引き続き「インフレが定着しないよう注力している」 としたうえで、 「そのため、 供給能力への圧力
緩和と一定期間にわたる需要抑制を通じてインフレ率を目標に戻す必要がある」 との認識を示した。
一方で、 「年初以降に実施した利上げにより金融環境は引き締まり、景気は減速している 」としつ
つ、 「インフレ率は依然として高過ぎる」 との認識を示した。今回の決定について 「足元の状況を踏
まえれば、 インフレ率を妥当な期間内に目標に収束させるには金融環境をさらに引き締めることが
妥当」との判断を示した。加えて、先行きも「インフレ率を持続的に目標水準に戻すために必要な
措置を引き続き講じる」 、 「必要であればさらなる利上げも含まれる」と追加利上げに言及した。先
行きの政策運営について「データと見通し、リスク評価を注視する」、「情勢変化に対応できる態勢

言を繰り返すなど、タカ派姿勢を維持する意向を示した。

【ブロック総裁は利上げと様子見の双方に言及､豪ドル相場の行方はどうなる？】 
会合後の記者会見に臨んだブロック総裁は、足元の物価動向について 「国内の供給能力のひっ迫
が影響している」 としたうえで、 「インフレ圧力が想定より長期化する可能性がある」 との認識を示
した。そして、 「超過需要の抑制を図る必要がある」 、 「基本シナリオでは景気後退に陥ることはな
い」との見通しを示した。今回の決定について「月次の物価統計そのものに重点を置いておらず、
金融環境を引き締める必要があると判断した」 、 「我々は金融環境が引き締め傾向にあると考える」
との見方を示しつつ、 「軽々しく決定した訳ではないが、インフレを抑えるためにやむを得ずそう
せざるを得なかった」とした。ただし、先行きについて「必要であれば利上げを実施する」としつ
つ、「慎重な引き締めを目指す」 として、 これまでの利上げペースに比べて緩やかなものとなる可能
性を示唆した。また、「物価統計の公表日に合わせて理事会の日程を調整したい」 とした。 一方で、
労働市場について「失業率は依然として歴史的低水準にある」、「雇用が失われることなく、失業率
が徐々に上昇する可能性はある」 としつつ、 「物価と賃金のスパイラル的な上昇はみられない」 とし
た。 
一方で、 「インフレが落ち着けば、 これ以上の利上げの必要性はなくなる」 、 「イラン情勢は大きな
衝撃であり、 我々を貧しくしている」 との認識も示した。 とはいえ、 「イラン情勢が長期化すればす
るほど、 企業がコストを転嫁する可能性は高まる」 として物価に与える影響を注視する考えを示し
た。足元で長期金利が上昇していることについて「債券市場は秩序ある動きをみせているが、注意
深く見守っている」 とした。 今回の協議について 「25bp の利上げか据え置きを協議した」 ことを明
らかにしたうえで、 「世界的なＡＩブームによるインフレリスクは高まりつつある 」 、 「インフレリ
スクのうち１つはすでに顕在化しており、 ２つは高まりつつある」 とした。「様々なインフレ圧力に
より極めて厳しい状況が生じている」との見方を示した。そのうえで、利上げが物価に与える影響
については「為替を通じたものになる」との認識を示した。

### Page 5

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
5/5 
このところの金融市場においては、豪ドルが強含む展開が続いてきた。日本と米国による協調介
入に加え、米国によるバイバック（長期国債の買い入れ消却）の倍増表明を受け、その後の米ドル
指数が軟調な動きを強めたため、豪ドル相場は一時２ヵ月半ぶりの高値をつけた。しかし、ＦＲＢ
（米連邦準備制度理事会）が９月の FOMC
（連邦公開市場委員会）で利上げを決定
したほか、その後も追加利上げに動くと
の観測を反映して、米ドル指数が持ち直
しの動きをみせており、豪ドル相場は足
元で下落に転じている（図４） 。 今回のＲ
ＢＡによる利上げ決定は、事前の市場予
想通りであり、声明文はタカ派姿勢の強
い内容であった。一方、ブロック総裁の
会見は、先行きの利上げ実施に含みを持
たせつつも、慎重に動く姿勢を示してお
り、声明文との間には幾分温度差があるようにみられる。このため、当面の豪ドル相場は上値の重
い展開が続く可能性があり、日本円に対しても同様の動きをみせると見込まれる。

## Document 329: The end of the safety net_ Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers (2026-09-28)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The end of the safety net_ Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers (2026-09-28).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

The end of the safety net:  
Life without the macroeconomic stabilisers

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

28 September 2026 
For several decades, financial markets and the wider economy benefited from an implicit 
safety net. If a slowdown or a downturn arrived, there was a monetary put from central 
banks, and a fiscal put from governments. The monetary put would see interest rates 
slashed and liquidity injected via QE. The fiscal put would see governments run larger 
deficits to cushion any downturn. These policy stabilisers smoothed out the business 
cycle, and supported asset valuations too. 
These policy interventions were increasingly normalised from the late-1980s, and became 
more aggressive with each passing cycle. This reached its peak during the Covid-19 
pandemic, when governments ran fiscal deficits unprecedented in peacetime, whilst 
many central banks expanded their balance sheets with huge asset purchases. 
But after an era of ever-bigger interventions, these old stabilisers now face major 
obstacles. On the fiscal side, policy is constrained today by sovereign yields at multi-year 
highs, alongside the highest debt-to-GDP ratios in several decades. Then on the monetary 
side, policy is constrained by persistent inflation that’s increasingly supply-driven. 
This represents a big shift from the last few decades, but it does echo previous regimes 
where the macroeconomic stabilisers were either blunted or left unused. A good example 
is the 1970s experience, when stagflation and supply shocks posed a dilemma to 
traditional policy tools. Even further back, the pre-WWI environment of the classical gold 
standard and balanced budget orthodoxy saw governments play a very different and more 
limited role in macroeconomic stabilisation. 
Looking forward, this environment means policymakers will have a much more difficult 
time stabilising the business cycle in the coming years. So absent a sharp fall in yields or a 
collapse in inflation, higher macro volatility will be here to stay.

### Page 2

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
1. Background: How the stabilisers used to work.
Economic policymakers have long used interventions to stabilise output. This
became increasingly fashionable after WWII, when Keynesian economics
established a more active use for fiscal policy to stimulate the economy in a
downturn. This meant budget deficits became a more regular feature of life, rather
than used solely for wartime emergencies.

It was from the late 1980s, however, that the extent of policy interventions became
increasingly sizeable. Indeed, it was here that the origins of a central bank “put” for
financial markets began, in the crisis of Black Monday in 1987. That was a huge
stock market crash, where the S&P 500 fell over 20% in a single day, marking the
biggest daily decline in its entire history. The size of the falls even went beyond the
Great Depression, raising fears of wider contagion, particularly if it were to spark a

liquidity crisis that caused financial institutions to fail. However, the Federal
Reserve responded by cutting rates and providing liquidity, offering a
macroeconomic stabiliser against the prospect of wider economic stress. This
offered support against a wider downturn, and the US economic expansion
continued until 1990.
Whilst the genesis of a central bank “put” came in 1987, the idea gained further
traction in the late-1990s, with the Asian financial crisis in 1997, followed by the

Russian financial crisis in 1998. Fears of an economic contraction mounted,
particularly after issues at Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) raised concerns
that the US wasn’t immune from these global pressures. So once again, the Fed
delivered three 25bp rate cuts pre-emptively, seeking to support the economy.
A few years later, the bursting of the dot com bubble in early 2000 set the stage for
yet further stimulus. As equity markets declined, the Fed began to cut rates from

early 2001, ultimately delivering 475bps of cuts that year that took rates from 6.5%
to just 1.75%. That was followed up by further rate cuts in 2002 and 2003, leaving
rates at 1% by mid-2003 into 2004. Moreover, the Bush tax cuts were also delivered
in 2001 and 2003, acting as a fiscal stimulus as the wider US economy entered
recession.
In hindsight, these moves seem modest compared to what then happened in 2008,
when the Global Financial Crisis led to even bigger policy interventions. Suddenly,

the world faced the biggest economic contraction since the Great Depression, with
genuine fears that a repeat might happen. To avoid this, policymakers launched into
the biggest economic stimulus ever seen in a peacetime environment. That
included massive fiscal stimulus, with the US federal deficit at 9.8% of GDP in 2008,
the highest since 1945. Moreover, central banks slashed rates to the zero lower
bound, and many like the Federal Reserve launched into Quantitative Easing

programmes, purchasing assets to ease financial conditions even more. Over the
coming years, many began pledging to keep rates low well into the future,
pioneering the concept of forward guidance.
These levels of policy intervention weren’t confined to the immediate crisis
however. In the subsequent years, active attempts were made to prolong and
extend the business cycle, particularly as the 2010s became an era of low inflation
and low interest rates. In this environment, borrowing was historically cheap, and

low inflation meant central banks could cut rates without seeing a big overshoot of
their targets. Indeed, both the Fed and the ECB undershot their 2% inflation target
on average in the 2010s, and the ECB took their own deposit rate into negative
territory in 2014.

### Page 3

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
Figure 1: The Federal Reserve and the ECB frequently missed their 2% inflation

By 2019, the year before the pandemic, the intellectual climate had continued to
move in the direction of more policy intervention. Government bond yields hit new

lows, with Germany’s 10-year bund yield at -0.7% in summer 2019. This
environment of negative yields meant borrowing was basically a “free lunch”, and
that summer also saw the Fed begin a 75bp easing cycle, as inflation was below
their target and they wanted to sustain the expansion.
The peak of this policy interventionism was seen with the Covid-19 pandemic in
2020. Just as in 2008, the world was facing a huge and sudden economic
contraction. Moreover, the consensus among policymakers was that they could

have done more to speed up the recovery after 2008, potentially avoiding years of
very high unemployment. As such, 2020 saw the most aggressive economic
stimulus packages ever seen in a peacetime environment, well above those in 2008.
Central banks immediately slashed rates back to the zero lower bound, pledged to
keep rates low for years to come, and launched into major QE programmes again.
Then on the fiscal side, record peacetime deficits were run. Indeed, the US federal

deficit of 14.5% of GDP in 2020 was the highest since 1945.
Figure 2: Federal Reserve Balance
Sheet (% of GDP)

### Page 4

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
During the pandemic period, many initially expected these interventions to remain
for years to come. After all, in mid-2020, financial markets continued to price a
continuation of the low inflation and low interest rates of the 2010s. This belief was
clear on several fronts, and the 10-year Treasury yield closed at just 0.51% in August
2020. Meanwhile, the Federal Reserve even adjusted its own framework to allow for

average inflation targeting, i.e. permitting an overshoot of the inflation target after
periods of an undershoot. This reflected the consensus view that inflation was
unlikely to be a serious threat.
But as we now know, inflation returned in a serious way after the pandemic, forcing
central banks into rate hikes. Meanwhile, public debt-to-GDP ratios rose
significantly thanks to the pandemic stimulus, helping to push up sovereign bond
yields too. In other words, many of the usual policy tools of the previous few

decades were either blunted or no longer available.
2. The Monetary Constraint: High inflation means the central
bank put isn’t available
In the pre-2020 world described above, central banks regularly grappled with how
to generate enough inflation. In part, this was thanks to several structural forces
that put downward pressure on global prices. For instance, deepening
globalisation acted as a positive supply shock that kept costs down. Meanwhile, the

expansion of the global labour force in these years also boosted the supply side. In
other words, inflation was low and central banks generally had the space to cut
interest rates when they needed to.
But since the pandemic, we have entered a very different environment of above-
target inflation across many of the world’s biggest economies. Initially, that was
caused by a huge wave of pent-up demand after the pandemic, alongside
disruption to supply chains and labour markets. But the subsequent years also

brought further shocks. Those included Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in early 2022,
driving a big spike in European energy prices, and pushing Euro Area inflation above
10% briefly. In 2026, the closure of the Strait of Hormuz then acted as a fresh energy
shock, pushing inflation further away from target and contributing to a fresh
tightening cycle from the major central banks.
Figure 4: In the 2020s, inflation has generally been above central bank targets

(PCE inflation for the US, HICP inflation for the Euro Area)
-2%
0%
2%
4%
6%
8%
10%
2019 2020 2021 2022 2023 2024 2025 2026
Federal Reserve ECB
Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank

### Page 5

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Alongside those shocks, the structural forces keeping inflation down have gone
into reverse. For instance, demographic trends have seen working-age populations
shrink in many places. Meanwhile, the trend towards deeper globalisation has
stalled, and the focus has turned to near-shoring and supply-chain resilience, rather
than locating production in the cheapest place. Simultaneously, other trends like

the push for higher defence spending are also adding to demand-side pressures.
Because of all this, the 2020s so far have brought persistently above-target inflation,
and central bankers have found it much more difficult to ease policy in response to
growth wobbles or financial stability fears.
This initially became apparent in 2022, amidst the biggest inflation wave for several
decades. Central banks had to keep hiking rates, even in the face of slowing growth

and financial market stress. Indeed, despite the S&P 500 being in bear market
territory by mid-2022, the Federal Reserve still launched into a series of 75bp hikes,
the biggest hikes since the 1990s. Then in 2023, despite the collapse of Silicon
Valley Bank and fears of wider financial contagion, the Fed and ECB still continued
with their rate hikes to combat inflation.
That tightening cycle in 2022-23 was a significant moment, because high inflation

meant monetary policy could not simply act as a stabiliser against growth or
financial stress. After all, policymakers realised that if they didn’t act to tackle high
inflation, they risked a repeat of the 1970s and the potential for even harsher
monetary tightening to get inflation under control again.
Today in 2026, this is a familiar problem again. We are in a globally synchronised
hiking cycle, with the Fed, ECB and Bank of Japan all having delivered rate hikes.

So far at least, global growth has been robust and financial conditions remain
accommodative by historical standards. But because inflation remains clearly
above target, central banks don’t have the space they did in the pre-pandemic world
to simply cut rates at the first sign of difficulty. There is still an inflation mandate to
be achieved, and given we have now seen multiple years of elevated inflation, there
is a much higher risk of inflation expectations becoming unanchored in that

context, particularly if another shock occurred.
3. The Fiscal Constraint: High yields and high public debt
mean fiscal stimulus isn’t easily available either
Just as monetary policy is constrained by high inflation, fiscal policy today is also
constrained by higher yields and higher public debt. This is a big transition from the
world of 1980-2020, when yields were generally falling throughout. Indeed, despite
all the fiscal stimulus around the GFC, the continuous decline in yields over the

2010s meant that debt dynamics remained manageable, because lower yields
helped to offset the impact of higher debt.
Since the pandemic however, we’ve been in a very different world, as the dual
forces of higher debt and higher yields have made debt dynamics far more
unfavourable around the world. And this isn’t just a nominal story, as real yields
have also risen to multi-year highs as well.

### Page 6

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
Figure 5: 10yr Government Bond Yields (%) have risen to multi-year highs across
several countries
-1
0
1

5

United States Germany France Japan UK
Source : Bloomberg Finance LP, Deutsche Bank
In essence, there are 4 new constraints around fiscal stimulus.
First, is the significant rise in global yields. By definition, the cost of incurring debt
has grown, in both nominal and real terms. Relative to the pre-pandemic baseline

in early 2020, the 10-year US Treasury yield has risen from 1.9% to 5.2%. Similarly
in Germany, the 10-year bund yield has risen from -0.2% to 3.6%. Moreover, with
yields already elevated, any further borrowing risks crowding out the private sector,
and further dampening growth. And for governments, interest spending has
ramped up significantly. For instance, US net interest spending as a share of GDP
exceeded its recent peak from the early 1990s in the most recent fiscal year, and the

CBO forecast this is set to continue.
Figure 6: US Net Interest Costs (% of GDP) with CBO Forecasts
0
1
2
3
4
5
6
7
1970 1980 1990 2000 2010 2020 2030 2040 2050
Source : CBO, Deutsche Bank
The second constraint is that public debt itself is now at a higher level. This interacts
with the higher yields mentioned above, because even though yields have only
returned to their levels from the mid-2000s, that is much more impactful because
they are being paid on a much larger debt pile. Using IMF numbers for consistency,

net public debt across the advanced economies has risen from 52% of GDP in 2000,
to 80% in 2025, with further increases projected to 2030. This means the fiscal
space available to governments is more limited than it used to be.

### Page 7

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7
The third constraint on fiscal stimulus is the high starting level of budget deficits. So
to generate a meaningful fiscal stimulus, it would require deficits to be pushed up
to even higher levels than they are today. But that in turn would add to the risks of
bond market disruption that cascades through the economy, leading to higher
borrowing costs for corporates and consumers as well.
The fourth constraint is higher inflation. When inflation is high, any fiscal stimulus

that boosts demand risks pushing up prices rather than boosting output, and in turn
makes monetary tightening more likely that could offset the impact of the stimulus.
This trade-off was apparent in 2022, when central banks were hiking rates
aggressively, and even further back in the inflationary environment of the 1970s. In
a world of high inflation and supply constraints, fiscal stimulus is more likely to
overheat the economy.
4. Are there any other eras of history where the stabilisers

weren’t available?
Whilst history never quite repeats exactly, there have been other eras in which the
traditional policy stabilisers were either unavailable or severely blunted, which hold
some lessons for our own time.
The pre-WWI classical gold standard
Before WWI, the international monetary system was known as the gold standard,
in which currencies were convertible into a fixed quantity of gold. In practice
however, this acted as a huge constraint on macroeconomic stabilisation. After all,

monetary policy had to maintain the convertibility of gold at a fixed parity. This
meant contemporary options like QE weren’t available in a crisis. Meanwhile on the
fiscal side, governments generally adhered to balanced budget orthodoxy. This was
before Keynes wrote the General Theory, so the modern understanding of
countercyclical deficit spending was not in place. In the US, there wasn’t even a
central bank until the Federal Reserve’s establishment in 1913.

Figure 7: Gold Prices (US Dollars per ounce, log scale)
1
10
100
1000
10000
1800 1850 1900 1950 2000
Under the classical gold standard,
monetary policy had to maintain

parity
Source : Finaeon, Deutsche Bank

### Page 8

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Without modern policy stabilisers however, this was also an era of huge
macroeconomic volatility. There was a major financial panic across Europe and the
US in 1873, which marked the start of the so-called “Long Depression”. In the US,
that contraction lasted until 1879, which in length was even longer than the Great
Depression contraction of 1929-33. And this was followed by other major panics,

including the 1893 panic that led to another US contraction, along with the 1907
panic that saw the Dow Jones almost halve relative to its 1906 peak.
The stagflationary 1970s
The more recent example comes from the 1970s, another era of multiple supply
shocks. The biggest were the two oil shocks in 1973 and 1979, which both
represented negative supply shocks that pushed up inflation and hurt growth in the
US and Europe. However, this meant policymakers faced a dilemma. If central

banks cut rates, that would add to the inflationary pressures and risk worsening the
wage-price spirals already underway. Meanwhile, if fiscal stimulus were pursued,
that would also push up inflation.
Figure 8: US CPI (year-on-year % change)
-2
0
2
4
6

1955 1960 1965 1970 1975 1980 1985
First Oil Shock
Second Oil Shock
Source : Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank
This context meant that policy couldn’t smooth out the business cycle as
successfully as it had in other decades. Indeed, in the decade from the first oil shock

in 1973, the US experienced 3 separate recessions. That is significantly more
turbulent than the last 40 years, when there have been just 4 recessions in total, or
once a decade on average. For financial markets too, the 1970s were a terrible
period for asset performance, with both equities and bonds losing ground in real
terms across multiple countries.
5. Implications
If economic policymakers can’t use traditional monetary and fiscal policy tools to

stabilise output and smooth the business cycle, then this will result in higher macro
volatility.
If we look back at the last 40 years, it is notable that the 5 most recent US expansions
all rank in the top 7 longest expansions since business-cycle dating began. This has
several causes, and some of it reflects a move away from an agriculture-based
economy where output was dependent on factors like the weather. But a key role
has also been played by pre-emptive policy interventions, such as cutting rates to

stop a slowdown turning into a recession. Indeed, recent examples include the a
dovish shifts in monetary policy after the 1987 stock market crash, the 1997-98

### Page 9

28 September 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
financial crises, and the mid-cycle slowdown of 2015-16.
Beyond the actual policy interventions, these moves also had a confidence effect
that supported financial markets. The fact that policymakers stood ready to take
action helped create a virtuous circle, as optimism on the economic outlook
boosted financial asset values, supported wealth effects, and avoided a tightening
in financial conditions that would itself cause growth to slow.

Clearly, policy is not as constrained today as in some periods of history. There is no

longer a gold standard where the convertibility needs to be maintained. And unlike
the 1970s, inflation is not in double-digits. Nevertheless, this is still a more
constrained world than we have been used to in the last 4 decades. Higher inflation
and more frequent supply shocks are constraining monetary policy. Meanwhile,
higher yields and higher public debt levels are constraining fiscal policy. We have
already seen the impacts since the pandemic, most notably in 2022, when central

banks hiked rates to combat the inflation surge, and there was a simultaneous
selloff across bonds and equities of a kind unseen in modern times.
Looking forward, this environment will offer new challenges of the sort we haven't
been used to in recent decades. The policy trade-offs are now more acute, and if
pre-emptive stabilisation is no longer available, then higher macro volatility lies
ahead.

U.S. macroeconomic performance has remained solid since the onset of the shock, as illustrated in particular by Q2 growth
of +3.8% and +9% (annualized quarterly rate) in consumer spending and private nonresidential investment, respectively.

In the manufacturing sector, business sentiment has gained momentum despite the shock, while the impact of this shock
on the services sector has remained very limited. The immediate reaction of consumer confidence was, however, more
negative before beginning to improve, which was nevertheless interrupted in August (latest available data for households’
sentiment on their future personal financial situation). The University of Michigan’s composite indicator continued to

deteriorate in September, due to the sharp rise in fuel prices. But declining confidence still does not translate into reduced
spending—at least not at the aggregate level.
On the inflation front, the spike immediately following the outbreak of the conflict in Iran was more pronounced than in
the months following Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. The decline in June still left inflation at a high level, but its stability in
July and August was notable (3.4% y/y), as was the latest—the third consecutive—slight decline in inflation excluding

energy in August (2.5% y/y, same rate as in February). Given the existing inflationary pressures, however, inflation is highly
likely to resume its upward trend in September.

## Document 363: What an evolving sustainability agenda means for US corporates

Source PDF: all report pdf/What an evolving sustainability agenda means for US corporates.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 6
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The quick answer: it is about future-prooﬁng businesses. As
extreme weather and AI reshape the future of sustainability,
resilience is becoming a business advantage. And resilience
and decarbonisation go hand in hand
Coco Zhang
Oregon, USA. As extreme weather and artiﬁcal intelligence reshape the
sustainability agenda, resilience is becoming a source of competitive
advantage
Rethinking business sustainability
This year’s New York Climate Week marked a clear shift from ﬁve

policy landscape have played a role, two broader trends are now
reshaping the sustainability agenda. Both were evident throughout
this year’s event.
First, climate risk got more attention. And for good reason.
Wildﬁres, hurricanes, and ﬂooding are becoming harder to ignore
and could cost the US over $1tr from 2026-2030.

### Page 2

Second, AI dominated the conversation. As electricity demand
surges, clean energy technologies such as solar, nuclear, and
geothermal are being called upon to expand supply, creating new
growth opportunities.
We expect these two forces to shape the future of sustainability in
the US. For corporates, the implication is clear: sustainability is
increasingly about future-prooﬁng the business.
What does that mean? It starts with managing climate risks.

Corporates need to secure access to the energy and resources
needed to operate and grow. They must also ensure their assets
can withstand a more volatile climate.
It also means capturing opportunities from clean technologies that
improve energy security and resilience. Because these solutions are
inherently low-carbon, they can support both business growth and
decarbonisation.
Below, we lay out how corporates in the US should treat these risks
and opportunities.

Climate risks and resilience should receive
more focus
The argument for corporates to manage climate risks is not new.
What has changed is urgency, which has grown exponentially, even
compared with just a few years ago.
Many companies in the US do acknowledge climate risks. But there
is a gap between acknowledgement and identifying them as
ﬁnancially material, and another gap between the latter and
making immediate investment decisions.
The result is that climate ﬁnance is skewed heavily toward reducing

emissions (mitigation), while climate risk management
(adaptation) receives far less. Annual global investment in
mitigation has reached $1.3tr-$1.8tr annually in recent years,
whereas adaptation investment stands only at $68bn per year.

### Page 3

Estimated breakdown of annual global
climate-related investment
Source: ING Research, World Economic Forum, Climate Policy Initiative
The underinvestment in managing climate risks makes companies
less prepared for climate events. This weighs diﬀerently across
sectors. Studies from S&P and MSCI have identiﬁed the utilities and
energy sectors as among the most vulnerable. This is largely
because of their extensive physical infrastructure. These assets are

hard to replace and highly susceptible to disruptions. In turn, even
localised failures can have wide-ranging impacts.
Next in line is the consumer goods sector. It is exposed to medium-
to-high climate risk, largely due to its often global and fragmented
supply chains. Climate-related disruptions aﬀecting suppliers can
quickly ripple through the sector.
Sectors such as industrials, materials, IT, and real estate are also
vulnerable, although the risks they face are less diﬃcult to contain

or transfer. Signiﬁcant losses can therefore still occur if climate risks
are not properly addressed.
To be fair, there are valid reasons behind the underinvestment in
climate risk. The “returns” from climate adaptation are still
consistently viewed as “loss avoided” rather than “revenues
generated.” By contrast, mitigation technologies such as solar
panels can produce tangible cash ﬂows and therefore attract
capital more easily.

### Page 4

But that assumption needs to be challenged. Research from various
insurers, rating agencies, banks, and government institutions
suggests that climate adaptation investments can generate
positive returns.
For example, ﬂood defences can reduce asset vulnerability, cooling
systems can improve operational reliability, and supplier
diversiﬁcation can increase supply chain resilience. They can make
businesses more durable and better positioned for growth.

The rise in demand for climate adaptation can also create new
business opportunities. Products such as climate-resilient
materials, including cross-laminated timber and multi-layered
glass, could see increasing adoption and investment.
Therefore, corporates that pursue the upside of managing climate
risks stand to win. They are more likely to beneﬁt from the broader
business value created by resilient infrastructure and products.
In an upcoming article, we will discuss the case for adopting more

ﬁnancing solutions to strengthen global corporate supply chain
resilience.
Clean technology is still essential, but its
deployment needs more than capital
The need to invest more in climate resilience does not diminish the
importance of decarbonisation. Without it, climate risks would only
become harder and more costly to manage.
Accelerating the deployment of clean technologies remains central
to decarbonisation. Beyond reducing emissions, clean energy helps

expand power supply. As US electricity demand rises with growing
AI data centres and manufacturing, technologies such as solar can
be deployed relatively quickly to meet demand. Over time,
emerging technologies like geothermal and longer-term solutions
such as nuclear could play a much larger role in the energy mix.
Corporates that pursue the upside of
managing climate risks stand to win.”
“

This is a clear example of how sustainability can support economic
growth. Corporates developing and supplying clean power are well
positioned to expand, while data centres and technology ﬁrms are
expected to remain major buyers of clean electricity and important
drivers of new capacity.
But other power-intensive industries may face increasing
competition from data centres for electricity, including clean power.
Aluminium is one example. Emirates Global Aluminium and

Century Aluminium are planning the ﬁrst new US aluminium
smelter in nearly 50 years, but securing suﬃcient electricity may
become more challenging as data centre demand continues to
grow. Securing reliable, aﬀordable clean power could therefore
become an increasingly important competitive advantage.
At the same time, advancing clean technologies is only part of the
solution. The next decade will be just as much about the
infrastructure needed to move and deliver that power. Today, grid

constraints can leave new generators and large electricity users
waiting more than four years for a connection, delaying investment
and expansion. Addressing these bottlenecks has become one of
the industry's deﬁning challenges and a key theme of New York
Climate Week.
One promising solution is greater ﬂexibility in electricity demand.
Companies that can adjust when and how they use power can help
free up grid capacity and, under many regulatory reforms, improve

their ability to connect to the system more quickly.
Encouragingly, more companies are exploring these approaches,
although eﬀorts remain fragmented. Even so, we may be
approaching a turning point where today’s best practices become
tomorrow’s industry standard, and today’s reforms help shape the
power system of the future.
Securing reliable, affordable clean power
could become an increasingly important
competitive advantage.”
